Continuing work from thread T-019c7289-a2ff-762d-bbde-80da4e731a2d. When you lack specific information you can use read_thread to get it.

@website/src/pages/showcase.astro @website/src/data/showcase.ts @website/astro.config.mjs

- I have a website built with Astro located in website/. The showcase page at website/src/pages/showcase.astro displays a gallery of editor screenshots with tag filtering and a lightbox.
- Screenshots are PNG files generated by a custom Rust binary (src/bin/screenshot.rs) and output directly into website/public/ as their serving location. They are referenced in website/src/data/showcase.ts with paths like '/screenshot-hero.png'.
- There are currently 30 screenshot scenarios generating PNGs at 2880x1800 or 3840x2160 (2x retina scale), so these files are likely quite large.
- The showcase page uses standard HTML img tags with loading="lazy" to display them. The lightbox also uses plain img tags.
- The site layout is in website/src/layouts/SiteLayout.astro, navigation in website/src/components/Navbar.astro.
- I want to explore whether Astro has built-in image optimization (responsive sizes, format conversion like WebP/AVIF, etc.) that could reduce page weight for the showcase gallery.


does astro have a way to optimize images that we could use?

import myImage from ' ../assets/my_image.png ' ; --- <!-- `alt` is mandatory on the Image component --> < Image src = { myImage } alt = \"\" class = \" my-class \" />\n```\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > `<Image />`\nContent:\n```\n<!-- Prerendered output --> < img src = \" /_astro/my_image.hash.webp \" width = \" 1600 \" height = \" 900 \" decoding = \" async \" loading = \" lazy \" class = \" my-class \" alt = \"\" />\n```\nTip\nYou can also use the `<Image />` component for images in the `public/` folder, or remote images not specifically configured in your project, even though these images will not be optimized or processed. The resulting image will be the same as using the HTML `<img>` .\nHowever, using the image component for all images provides a consistent authoring experience and prevents Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS) even for your unoptimized images.\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > `<Picture />`\nContent:\nSection titled “”\n**Added in:** `astro@3.3.0`\nUse the built-in `<Picture />` Astro component to generate a `<picture>` tag with multiple formats and/or sizes of your image. This allows you to specify preferred file formats to display and at the same time, provide a fallback format. Like the `<Image />` component , images will be processed at build time for prerendered pages. When your page is rendered on demand, processing will occur on the fly when the page is viewed.\nThe following example uses the `<Picture />` component to transform a local `.png` file into a web-friendly `avif` and `webp` format as well as the `.png` `<img>` that can be displayed as a fallback when needed:\nsrc/pages/index.astro\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > `<Picture />`\nContent:\n```\n--- import { Picture } from ' astro:assets ' ; import myImage from ' ../assets/my_image.png ' ; // Image is 1600x900 --- <!-- `alt` is mandatory on the Picture component --> < Picture src = { myImage } formats = { [ ' avif ' , ' webp ' ] } alt = \" A description of my image. \" />\n```\n```\n<!-- Prerendered output --> < picture > < source srcset = \" /_astro/my_image.hash.avif \" type = \" image/avif \" /> < source srcset = \" /_astro/my_image.hash.webp \" type = \" image/webp \" /> < img src = \" /_astro/my_image.hash.png \" width = \" 1600 \" height = \" 900 \" decoding = \" async \" loading = \" lazy \" alt = \" A description of my image. \" /> </ picture >\n```\nSee details about the `<Picture />` component properties in the `astro:assets` reference.\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > Responsive image behavior\nContent:\nSection titled “Responsive image behavior”\n**Added in:** `astro@5.10.0`\nResponsive images are images that adjust to improve performance across different devices. These images can resize to fit their container, and can be served in different sizes depending on your visitor’s screen size and resolution.\nWith responsive image properties applied to the `<Image />` or `<Picture />` components, Astro will automatically generate the required `srcset` and `sizes` values for your images, and apply the necessary styles to ensure they resize correctly .\nWhen this responsive behavior is configured globally with `image.layout` , it will apply to all image components and also to any local and remote images using the Markdown `!` syntax .\nImages in your `public/` folder are never optimized, and responsive images are not supported.\nNote\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > Responsive image behavior\nContent:\nA single responsive image will generate multiple images of different sizes so that the browser can show the best one to your visitor.\nFor prerendered pages, this happens during the build and may increase the build time of your project, especially if you have a large number of images.\nFor pages rendered on-demand, the images are generated as-needed when a page is visited. This has no impact on build times but may increase the number of image transformations performed when an image is displayed. Depending on your image service this may incur additional costs.\nRead more about [responsive images on MDN web docs](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTML/Guides/Responsive_images) .\nSection Title: Images > ... > Responsive image behavior > Generated HTML output for responsive images\nContent:\nSection titled “Generated HTML output for responsive images”\nWhen a layout is set, either by default or on an individual component, images have automatically generated `srcset` and `sizes` attributes based on the image’s dimensions and the layout type. Images with `constrained` and `full-width` layouts will have styles applied to ensure they resize according to their container.\nsrc/components/MyComponent.astro\n```\n--- import { Image } from ' astro:assets ' ; import myImage from ' ../assets/my_image.png ' ; --- < Image src = { myImage } alt = \" A description of my image. \" layout = ' constrained ' width = { 800 } height = { 600 } />\n```\nThis `<Image />` component will generate the following HTML output on a prerendered page:\nSection Title: Images > ... > Responsive image behavior > Generated HTML output for responsive images\nContent:\n```\n< img src = \" /_astro/my_image.hash3.webp \" srcset = \" /_astro/my_image.hash1.webp 640w, /_astro/my_image.hash2.webp 750w, /_astro/my_image.hash3.webp 800w, /_astro/my_image.hash4.webp 828w, /_astro/my_image.hash5.webp 1080w, /_astro/my_image.hash6.webp 1280w, /_astro/my_image.hash7.webp 1600w \" alt = \" A description of my image \" sizes = \" (min-width: 800px) 800px, 100vw \" loading = \" lazy \" decoding = \" async \" fetchpriority = \" auto \" width = \" 800 \" height = \" 600 \" style = \" --fit: cover; --pos: center; \" data-astro-image = \" constrained \" >\n```\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > Responsive image behavior > Responsive image styles\nContent:\nSection titled “Responsive image styles”\nSetting `image.responsiveStyles: true` applies a small number of global styles to ensure that your images resize correctly. In most cases, you will want to enable these as a default; your images will not be responsive without additional styles.\nHowever, if you prefer to handle responsive image styling yourself, or need to override these defaults when using Tailwind 4 , leave the default `false` value configured.\nThe global styles applied by Astro will depend on the layout type, and are designed to produce the best result for the generated `srcset` and `sizes` attributes. These are the default styles:\nResponsive Image Styles\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > Responsive image behavior > Responsive image styles\nContent:\n```\n:where ([ data-astro-image ]) { object-fit : var ( --fit ); object-position : var ( --pos ); } :where ([ data-astro-image = ' full-width ' ]) { width : 100 % ; } :where ([ data-astro-image = ' constrained ' ]) { max-width : 100 % ; }\n```\nThe styles use the [`:where()` pseudo-class](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/:where) , which has a [specificity](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/CSS_cascade/Specificity) of 0, meaning that it is easy to override with your own styles. Any CSS selector will have a higher specificity than `:where()` , so you can easily override the styles by adding your own styles to target the image.\nYou can override the `object-fit` and `object-position` styles on a per-image basis by setting the `fit` and `position` props on the `<Image />` or `<Picture />` component.\nSection Title: Images > Astro components for images > Responsive image behavior > Responsive images with Tailwind 4\nContent:\nSection titled “Responsive images with Tailwind 4”\nTailwind 4 is compatible with Astro’s default responsive styles. However, Tailwind uses [cascade layers](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/@layer) , meaning that its rules are always lower specificity than rules that don’t use layers, including Astro’s responsive styles. Therefore, Astro’s styling will take precedence over Tailwind styling. To use Tailwind rules instead of Astro’s default styling, do not enable Astro’s default responsive styles ."

      "Section Title: ... > Overview of Astro.js's Built-In Image Optimization Features\nContent:\nAstro.js offers a comprehensive set of image optimization features out of the box, making it easier for developers to deliver high-performance websites. Let's take a look at some of the key features:\nSection Title: ... > Overview of Astro.js's Built-In Image Optimization Features\nContent:\n**Automatic Image Optimization** : Astro.js automatically optimizes images during the build process. It applies techniques such as compression, resizing, and format conversion to reduce image file sizes without compromising quality. **Lazy Loading** : Astro.js supports lazy loading of images, which means images are loaded only when they come into the viewport. This technique improves page load times. **Responsive Images** : With Astro.js, you can easily generate responsive images that adapt to different screen sizes and device resolutions. **Image Formats** : Astro.js supports modern image formats like WebP, which offer better compression and smaller file sizes compared to traditional formats like JPEG or PNG. This helps further optimize image delivery.\nSection Title: ... > Overview of Astro.js's Built-In Image Optimization Features\nContent:\n**Integration with Headless CMS** : Astro.js seamlessly integrates with [headless content management systems (CMS)](https://caisy.io/blog/what-is-a-headless-cms) like caisy. By leveraging a headless CMS, developers can efficiently manage and deliver optimized images to their Astro.js projects. Caisy provides a user-friendly interface for managing images, while Astro.js handles the optimization and delivery aspects, creating a powerful combination for building high-performance websites. Here's a guide to [integrating Astro with caisy](https://caisy.io/developer/docs/examples/astro-example) .\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > Using Astro.js's Image and Picture Components\nContent:\nAstro.js provides two powerful built-in components for handling images: `<Image />` and `<Picture />` . These components offer a range of features and optimizations to ensure that your images load quickly and efficiently across different devices and screen sizes.\nSection Title: ... > The `<Image />` Component: Optimizing Local and Remote Images\nContent:\nThe `<Image />` component is designed to optimize and display both local and remote images in your Astro.js project. It automatically applies image optimization techniques, such as format conversion (e.g., WebP) and compression, to reduce file sizes and improve loading times.\nTo use the `<Image />` component, simply import it from `astro:assets` and provide the necessary properties:\n`import { Image } from 'astro:assets';`\n`<Image src=\"path/to/image.jpg\" alt=\"Description\" width={500} height={300} />`\nThe component accepts various properties, including `src` , `alt` , `width` , `height` , `format` , `quality` , and more. By specifying the `width` and `height` , Astro.js can prevent layout shifts and ensure a smooth user experience.\nSection Title: ... > The `<Picture />` Component: Delivering Responsive Images\nContent:\nFor scenarios where you need to serve different image formats or sizes based on the user's device capabilities, Astro.js offers the `<Picture />` component. This component allows you to define multiple image sources and let the browser choose the most appropriate one.\nHere's an example of using the `<Picture />` component:\n`import { Picture } from 'astro:assets';`\n```\n<Picture>\n  <source type=\"image/avif\" srcset=\"image.avif\" />\n  <source type=\"image/webp\" srcset=\"image.webp\" />\n  <img src=\"image.jpg\" alt=\"Description\" />\n</Picture>\n```\nIn this example, the browser will prioritize the AVIF format if supported, followed by WebP, and fallback to the default JPG image if neither is available. This approach ensures that users receive the most optimized image format their browser can handle.\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Configuring Image Optimization Settings\nContent:\nAstro.js provides a set of configuration options to customize the image optimization process. By default, Astro uses the Sharp image processing library, but you can switch to Squoosh or even a custom image service if needed. Here's an example of how to configure the image optimization settings in your `astro.config.mjs` file:\n```\nimport { defineConfig } from 'astro/config';\n\nexport default defineConfig({\n  image: {\n    service: 'sharp',\n    serviceEntryPoint: '@astrojs/image/sharp',\n    logLevel: 'info',\n    cacheDir: './.cache/image',\n    defaultFormat: 'webp',\n  },\n});\n```\nIn this configuration, we specify the image service ( `sharp` ), the entry point for the service ( `@astrojs/image/sharp` ), the log level ( `info` ), the cache directory ( `./.cache/image` ), and the default image format ( `webp` ). You can adjust these settings based on your project's requirements and performance needs.\nSection Title: ... > Leveraging the getImage() Function for Programmatic Optimization\nContent:\nIn addition to using the `<Image />` component in your Astro pages, you can also optimize images programmatically using the `getImage()` function. This is particularly useful when you need to generate optimized images dynamically or outside of the Astro component context. Here's an example:\n```\nimport { getImage } from '@astrojs/image';\n\nconst optimizedImage = await getImage({\n  src: '/path/to/image.jpg',\n  width: 800,\n  height: 600,\n  format: 'webp',\n  quality: 80,\n});\n```\nThe `getImage()` function accepts an object with various properties, such as the image source ( `src` ), desired width and height, format, and quality. It returns an optimized image object that you can use in your application logic or API responses.\n ... \nSection Title: ... > Integrating with Third-Party Image Optimization Services\nContent:\nOnce configured, you can use the `<Image />` component with Cloudinary-specific properties to optimize and deliver images through their service:\n```\n---\nimport { Image } from '@astrojs/cloudinary';\n---\n\n<Image\n  src=\"your-image-public-id\"\n  width={800}\n  height={600}\n  format=\"webp\"\n  quality={80}\n  alt=\"Your image description\"\n/>\n```\nBy integrating with third-party services, you can leverage their advanced optimization algorithms, automatic format selection, and global content delivery network to ensure optimal performance for your images.\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Referencing Images in Markdown and MDX Files\nContent:\nIn Markdown ( `.md` ) and MDX ( `.mdx` ) files, you can reference images using the standard Markdown syntax:\n```\n!Alt Text\n```\nAstro will automatically optimize these images, whether they are local or from authorized remote sources. For local images, make sure to place them in the `src/` folder or `public/` directory.\nWhen using MDX, you can also leverage Astro's `<Image />` and `<Picture />` components directly in your content:\n```\nimport { Image } from 'astro:assets';\n\n<Image src={localImage} alt=\"description\" />\n```\nThis allows for more advanced image optimization and responsive image handling.\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Best Practices for Managing Images in Content\nContent:\nWhen working with images in your content, consider the following best practices:\nUse descriptive `alt` text for accessibility. For decorative images, set `alt=\"\"` .\nOptimize images before adding them to your project to reduce file size.\nLeverage responsive image techniques, such as using the `<Picture />` component or specifying `widths` and `sizes` for the `<Image />` component.\nConsider using a Content Delivery Network (CDN) or third-party image service for better performance and scalability.\nRegularly audit your content images to ensure they are properly optimized and accessible.\nBy following these best practices and leveraging Astro.js's built-in image optimization features, you can ensure that your content images are performant, accessible, and visually appealing. Another best practice is integrating [Astro with a Headless CMS](https://caisy.io/developer/docs/examples/astro-example) .\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Planning and Designing Your Image Gallery\nContent:\nBefore diving into the code, it's crucial to plan and design your image gallery. Consider the following factors:\nLayout: Decide on the overall layout of your gallery. Will it be a grid, a masonry-style layout, or a carousel? Think about how you want to present your images and what will provide the best user experience.\nResponsiveness: Ensure that your gallery is responsive and adapts well to different screen sizes. Consider using CSS media queries and flexible layouts to achieve a seamless experience across devices.\nAccessibility: Keep accessibility in mind when designing your gallery. Use appropriate alt text for images, ensure keyboard navigation is possible, and consider contrast ratios for text overlays.\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Implementing a Responsive Image Grid\nContent:\n```\n---\nimport { Image } from '@astrojs/image/components';\n\nconst images = [\n  { src: 'image1.jpg', alt: 'Image 1' },\n  { src: 'image2.jpg', alt: 'Image 2' },\n  // Add more image objects as needed\n];\n---\n\n<div class=\"gallery-grid\">\n  {images.map((image) => (\n    <div class=\"gallery-item\">\n      <Image src={image.src} alt={image.alt} width={400} height={400} />\n    </div>\n  ))}\n</div>\n```\nCustomize the styles of the grid items to achieve the desired look and feel of your gallery. You can add borders, shadows, or hover effects to enhance the visual appeal.\n ... \nSection Title: Mastering Image Optimization in Astro.js > ... > Optimizing Image Delivery with CDNs and Astro.js\nContent:\nOnce you have configured your chosen CDN with Astro.js, you can start optimizing your image delivery. The `@astrojs/image` package provides an `<Image>` component that you can use to display optimized images in your Astro pages.\nHere's an example of using the `<Image>` component:\n```\n---\nimport { Image } from '@astrojs/image/components';\n---\n\n<Image \n  src=\"path/to/your/image.jpg\"\n  alt=\"Description of the image\"\n  width={500}\n  height={300}\n  format=\"webp\"\n/>\n```\nThe `<Image>` component automatically optimizes the image based on the specified `width` , `height` , and `format` props. It also generates appropriate HTML markup for responsive images and lazy loading.\nBy leveraging the power of image CDNs and the `@astrojs/image` package, you can significantly improve the performance of your Astro.js websites, delivering optimized images to your users faster and more efficiently.\n ... \nSection Title: ... > Applying Image Optimization Best Practices to Your Astro.js Projects\nContent:\nAs you embark on your next Astro.js project, keep the following best practices in mind:\nChoose the appropriate image formats (e.g., WebP, AVIF) for optimal compression and browser support\nImplement responsive images to deliver the best-suited image size for each device\nLeverage lazy loading to improve initial page load times\nOptimize images before uploading them to your project\nConsider using image CDNs for faster delivery and reduced server load\nAs you've seen throughout this guide, Astro.js provides a powerful set of tools and techniques for optimizing images in your web projects. However, managing images is just one aspect of building high-performance, user-friendly websites. This is where the headless CMS caisy shines."

    "excerpts": [
      "[](https://astro.build/) \n\nSearch\n\n[GitHub](https://github.com/withastro/astro) [Discord](https://astro.build/chat)\n\nSelect theme Dark Light Auto Select language English Deutsch Português do Brasil Español 简体中文 正體中文 Français हिन्दी العربية 日本語 한국어 Polski Русский Italiano\n\n## Sponsored by\n\n[](https://www.netlify.com/?utm_campaign=Astro-2024&utm_source=astro-referral) [](https://sentry.io/welcome/?utm_medium=partner&utm_source=astro&utm_campaign=astro-docs) [](https://idx.dev/?utm_source=astro&utm_medium=astro&utm_campaign=astro)\n\n[GitHub](https://github.com/withastro/astro) [Discord](https://astro.build/chat)\n\nSelect theme Dark Light Auto Select language English Deutsch Português do Brasil Español 简体中文 正體中文 Français हिन्दी العربية 日本語 한국어 Polski Русский Italiano\n\n## Sponsored by\n\n[](https://www.netlify.com/?utm_campaign=Astro-2024&utm_source=astro-referral) [](https://sentry.io/welcome/?utm_medium=partner&utm_source=astro&utm_campaign=astro-docs) [](https://idx.dev/?utm_source=astro&utm_medium=astro&utm_campaign=astro)\n\n## On this page\n\n* Overview\n* Responsive image properties\n* Responsive image configuration settings\n\n[## Want to build your own Docs?](https://starlight.astro.build/)\n\nGrab this template to get started. **→**\n\n# Experimental responsive images\n\n**Type:** `boolean`  \n**Default:** `false`\n\n**Added in:** `astro@5.0.0`\n\nEnables support for automatic responsive images in your project.\n\nastro.config.mjs\n\n```\n{ experimental: { responsiveImages: true , }, }\n```\n\nWhen this flag is enabled, you can pass a `layout` prop to any `<Image />` or `<Picture />` component to create a responsive image.\n\nWhen a layout is set, images have automatically generated `srcset` and `sizes` attributes based on the image’s dimensions and the layout type. Images with `responsive` and `full-width` layouts will have styles applied to ensure they resize according to their container.\n\nMyComponent.astro\n\n```\n--- import { Image, Picture } from ' astro:assets ' ; import myImage from ' ../assets/my_image.png ' ; --- < Image src = { myImage } alt = \" A description of my image. \" layout = ' responsive ' width = { 800 } height = { 600 } /> < Picture src = { myImage } alt = \" A description of my image. \" layout = ' full-width ' formats = { [ ' avif ' , ' webp ' , ' jpeg ' ] } />\n```\n\nThis `<Image />` component will generate the following HTML output:\n\n```\n< img src = \" /_astro/my_image.hash3.webp \" srcset = \" /_astro/my_image.hash1.webp 640w, /_astro/my_image.hash2.webp 750w, /_astro/my_image.hash3.webp 800w, /_astro/my_image.hash4.webp 828w, /_astro/my_image.hash5.webp 1080w, /_astro/my_image.hash6.webp 1280w, /_astro/my_image.hash7.webp 1600w \" alt = \" A description of my image \" sizes = \" (min-width: 800px) 800px, 100vw \" loading = \" lazy \" decoding = \" async \" fetchpriority = \" auto \" width = \" 800 \" height = \" 600 \" style = \" --w: 800; --h: 600; --fit: cover; --pos: center; \" data-astro-image = \" responsive \" >\n```\n\nThe following styles are applied to ensure the images resize correctly:\n\nResponsive Image Styles\n\n```\n[ data-astro-image ] { width : 100 % ; height : auto ; object-fit : var ( --fit ); object-position : var ( --pos ); aspect-ratio : var ( --w ) / var ( --h ) } [ data-astro-image = responsive ] { max-width : calc ( var ( --w ) * 1 px ); max-height : calc ( var ( --h ) * 1 px ) } [ data-astro-image = fixed ] { width : calc ( var ( --w ) * 1 px ); height : calc ( var ( --h ) * 1 px ) }\n```\n\n## Responsive image properties\n\nSection titled Responsive image properties\n\nThese are additional properties available to the `<Image />` and `<Picture />` components when responsive images are enabled:\n\n* `layout` : The layout type for the image. Can be `responsive` , `fixed` , `full-width` or `none` . Defaults to value of `image.experimentalLayout` .\n* `fit` : Defines how the image should be cropped if the aspect ratio is changed. Values match those of CSS `object-fit` . Defaults to `cover` , or the value of `image.experimentalObjectFit` if set.\n* `position` : Defines the position of the image crop if the aspect ratio is changed. Values match those of CSS `object-position` . Defaults to `center` , or the value of `image.experimentalObjectPosition` if set.\n* `priority` : If set, eagerly loads the image. Otherwise images will be lazy-loaded. Use this for your largest above-the-fold image. Defaults to `false` .\n\nThe `widths` and `sizes` attributes are automatically generated based on the image’s dimensions and the layout type, and in most cases should not be set manually. The generated `sizes` attribute for `responsive` and `full-width` images\nis based on the assumption that the image is displayed at close to the full width of the screen when the viewport is smaller than the image’s width. If it is significantly different (e.g. if it’s in a multi-column layout on small screens) you may need to adjust the `sizes` attribute manually for best results.\n\nThe `densities` attribute is not compatible with responsive images and will be ignored if set.\n\n## Responsive image configuration settings\n\nSection titled Responsive image configuration settings\n\nYou can enable responsive images for all `<Image />` and `<Picture />` components by setting `image.experimentalLayout` with a default value. This settings can be overridden by the `layout` prop on each component.\n\nastro.config.mjs\n\n```\n{ image: { // Used for all `<Image />` and `<Picture />` components unless overridden experimentalLayout: ' responsive ' , }, experimental: { responsiveImages: true , }, }\n```\n\nWith `responsive` set as the default layout, you can override any individual image’s `layout` property:\n\n```\n--- import { Image } from ' astro:assets ' ; import myImage from ' ../assets/my_image.png ' ; --- < Image src = { myImage } alt = \" This will use responsive layout \" width = { 800 } height = { 600 } /> < Image src = { myImage } alt = \" This will use full-width layout \" layout = \" full-width \" /> < Image src = { myImage } alt = \" This will disable responsive images \" layout = \" none \" />\n```\n\nFor a complete overview, and to give feedback on this experimental API, see the [Responsive Images RFC](https://github.com/withastro/roadmap/blob/responsive-images/proposals/0053-responsive-images.md) .\n\nReference\n\n[Edit page](https://github.com/withastro/docs/edit/main/src/content/docs/en/reference/experimental-flags/responsive-images.mdx) [Translate this page](https://contribute.docs.astro.build/guides/i18n/)\n\nPrevious Configuring experimental flags Next SVG components\n\nContribute Give us feedback [Community](https://community.astro.build)"
    ]

]

## Assistant

Yes, Astro has excellent built-in image optimization. You're on Astro 5, which includes:

1. **`<Image />`** — converts to WebP by default, adds `width`/`height`/`loading="lazy"`, prevents CLS
2. **`<Picture />`** — generates `<picture>` with multiple `<source>` tags for AVIF + WebP with PNG/JPG fallback
3. **Responsive images** (stable in 5.10+) — auto-generates `srcset`/`sizes` with `layout="constrained"` or `layout="full-width"`

**Key caveat for your setup:** Your screenshots live in `public/` and are referenced by absolute path (`/screenshot-hero.png`). Images in `public/` are **not optimized** by Astro — they're copied as-is. To get optimization, you'd need to either:

- **Move images to `src/assets/`** and import them (e.g., `import hero from '../assets/screenshot-hero.png'`), then pass the import to `<Image src={hero} />` or `<Picture />`
- Or keep them in `public/` but use `<Image src="/screenshot-hero.png" width={2880} height={1800} />` — this gives you CLS prevention but **no format conversion or resizing** for local public images

The recommended approach would be moving the screenshots into `src/assets/` and using `<Picture formats={['avif', 'webp']} />` in the showcase grid and lightbox. This would auto-generate optimized AVIF/WebP versions at build time. Want me to implement this?

Now I need to understand the approach. The challenge here is that the showcase page has a lightbox with dynamic `src` switching via JavaScript — `<Picture>` can't help there since `srcset` is baked into `<source>` elements at build time. Let me think through the best approach:

- **Grid thumbnails**: Use `<Picture>` with `formats={['avif', 'webp']}` for the grid cards — these are static and known at build time ✓
- **Lightbox**: The lightbox dynamically swaps `img.src` via JS. We need the optimized URLs available in JS. We can use `getImage()` from `astro:assets` to get the optimized image URLs at build time, then pass those to the client-side JS.
- **index.astro**: Also uses 2 screenshots with plain `<img>` — should be updated too.
- **Screenshot binary**: Change `--out-dir` default from `website/public` to `website/src/assets/screenshots`
- **Cleanup**: Remove `screenshot-card-*` files (unused) and the old `public/screenshot-*` files after moving

## Assistant

Done. Here's what changed:

- **Screenshots moved** from `website/public/` → `website/src/assets/screenshots/` so Astro processes them
- **Grid cards** now use `<Picture formats={['avif', 'webp']} widths={[400, 600, 800]}>` — browsers pick the smallest format/size they support
- **Lightbox** uses `getImage()` to pre-generate WebP versions at build time, passed to the client JS via `data-items`
- **Index page** hero + splits images also converted to `<Picture>`
- **Screenshot binary** default `--out-dir` updated to `website/src/assets/screenshots`
- **Deleted** 3 unused `screenshot-card-*.png` files (~1.1MB)

At build time, Astro generates 351 optimized variants (AVIF → WebP → PNG fallback at multiple widths). A browser supporting AVIF will load ~4-90KB per grid thumbnail instead of the original 300-2200KB PNGs.

## 1) “See it in action” — micro-demos with a narrative (most compelling)
A lightweight section that turns the current “floating screenshot” into 3 tight, scrollable “moments” that answer: *what does it feel like to use Token?*

- **Layout:** Heading + 3 small featurettes (each = short blurb + screenshot in same nice frame style you already have).
- **Suggested moments (map to your unused features):**
  1) **Preview-first writing**: “Markdown & HTML preview, instant feedback.”
     Use `screenshot-showcase-markdown-preview.png` or `screenshot-showcase-html-preview.png`.
  2) **Command-driven workflow**: “Command palette for everything (themes, actions).”
     Use `screenshot-showcase-command-palette.png`.
  3) **Fast navigation & edits**: “Find/replace + go to line for quick refactors.”
     Use `screenshot-showcase-find-replace.png` + `screenshot-showcase-goto-line.png` (either as two stacked mini images or a simple tabbed/segmented control).

**Why it works (like Zed/Sublime):** They sell *flow*—a few crisp, confident visuals with one-sentence claims. It adds meaning without adding a giant wall of text.

---

## 2) “Workspace that stays out of your way” — sidebar + project story
A single, calm section that frames Token as a *real project editor*, not just a text buffer.

- **Layout:** Heading + 2-column: left = copy bullets, right = `screenshot-showcase-sidebar-splits.png` (or `screenshot-showcase-sidebar.png`).
- **Copy angle:** “Open a folder. Jump between files. Split and focus.”
  Bullet points could be:
  - Workspace tree for real projects
  - Splits for reading/editing across files
  - Minimal chrome, maximum code

**Why it works (like VS Code’s positioning, but minimal):** Visitors often ask “can it handle a project?” This answers immediately with one image and 3 bullets.

---

## 3) “Make it yours” — themes + personalization (high conversion, low bloat)
A compact section that turns your theme system into a “taste” hook—people download editors they like looking at.

- **Layout:** Heading + small grid of theme chips (Dracula/Nord/Gruvbox/etc.) + one hero screenshot that changes (optional), or just a tasteful mosaic of 3–4 thumbnails.
- **Screenshot options:** Use 3–4 theme screenshots, or pair theme picker modal (`screenshot-showcase-command-palette.png`) with a themed editor shot.
- **Copy angle:** “9 built-in themes. Switch in seconds.”

**Why it works (common on Zed/VS Code pages):** It’s emotional/visual proof, and it’s extremely skimmable.

---

## 4) “Built for speed (and focus)” — short principles section (credibility boost)
You already have stats, but this is a *values* section that explains the why in one screen—without turning into a manifesto.

- **Layout:** Heading + 3 principle cards (small, iconless, clean):
  - **Native & lightweight:** “Rust-first. No Electron.”
  - **Instant response:** “Startup and actions tuned for low latency.”
  - **Minimal UI:** “Everything you need, nothing you don’t.”
- **Visual:** Use your best “wow” screenshot as subtle support (e.g., `screenshot-showcase-fractal-splits.png`) or keep it text-only with a thin divider.

**Why it works (like Helix/Sublime):** Those pages sell philosophy and performance. This section reassures skeptical devs that the editor is intentionally engineered.

---

### My primary recommendation (simple, high impact)
Replace the lone screenshot with **Section #1 (“See it in action” micro-demos)** using **Markdown preview**, **command palette**, and **find/replace + go-to-line**. It’s the best conversion-per-pixel: it shows multiple real workflows, uses your existing assets, and adds context without feeling like bloat.

**Effort:** S–M (1–3h) depending on whether you add tabs/segmented controls vs. just stacking 3 featurettes.

  "diff": "```diff\nIndex: ~/code/token-editor/website/src/pages/index.astro\n===================================================================\n--- ~/code/token-editor/website/src/pages/index.astro\toriginal\n+++ ~/code/token-editor/website/src/pages/index.astro\tmodified\n@@ -1179,5 +1179,34 @@\n     await navigator.clipboard.writeText('curl -fsSL token-editor.com/install.sh | sh');\n     btn.textContent = '✓';\n     setTimeout(() => btn.textContent = '⎘', 1500);\n   });\n+\n+  // Showcase image swapper\n+  const showcaseEl = document.getElementById('showcase-hero');\n+  if (showcaseEl) {\n+    const slides: { id: string; label: string; src: string }[] = JSON.parse(showcaseEl.dataset.slides || '[]');\n+    const img = document.getElementById('showcase-img') as HTMLImageElement;\n+    const imgWrap = showcaseEl.querySelector('.showcase-hero-img') as HTMLElement;\n+    const pills = showcaseEl.querySelectorAll('.showcase-pill');\n+\n+    pills.forEach((pill) => {\n+      pill.addEventListener('click', () => {\n+        const slideId = (pill as HTMLElement).dataset.slide;\n+        const slide = slides.find((s) => s.id === slideId);\n+        if (!slide || img.src.endsWith(slide.src)) return;\n+\n+        pills.forEach((p) => p.classList.remove('active'));\n+        pill.classList.add('active');\n+\n+        imgWrap.classList.add('loading');\n+        const next = new Image();\n+        next.onload = () => {\n+          img.src = slide.src;\n+          img.alt = `Token editor — ${slide.label}`;\n+          imgWrap.classList.remove('loading');\n+        };\n+        next.src = slide.src;\n+      });\n+    });\n+  }\n </script>\n```",

The find & replace modal rendering is located in `src/view/mod.rs` within the `render_modals` function. Its geometry calculations are split between `src/view/geometry.rs` (base bounds) and `src/view/mod.rs` (conditional height adjustments).

- **Bounds Calculation**: The `modal_bounds` function in `geometry.rs` calculates the base width (clamped 300-500px), centered X position, and a Y offset capped at 100px.
- **Height Calculation**:
    - **Base Height**: Set in `geometry.rs` as `line_height * 3 + 20`.
    - **Find & Replace Extension**: In `mod.rs#L2180-L2188`, if `replace_mode` is active, an `extra_height` (`line_height + 12 + line_height + 4`) is added to the base height to accommodate the additional label and input field.
- **Rendering**: The modal draws a dimmed background, a bordered rectangle for the dialog, and then uses `TextFieldRenderer` for the individual input fields.

Relevant files:
- [src/view/mod.rs#L2179-L2300](file://~/code/token-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L2179-L2300) (Find & Replace specific rendering logic)
- [src/view/mod.rs#L1911-L1950](file://~/code/token-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L1911-L1950) (General modal setup and theme colors)

## Assistant

Found the bug. Let me trace the math:

- `base_height = line_height * 3 + 20` (from `modal_bounds`)
- `extra_height = line_height + 12 + line_height + 4` (when `replace_mode` is true)

But the actual content rendered is:
- Title: `title_y = modal_y + 8` → title takes `line_height`
- "Find:" label: `find_label_y = title_y + line_height + 8` → another `line_height`
- Find input: `find_input_y = find_label_y + line_height + 4` → input is `line_height + 8` tall
- "Replace:" label: `replace_label_y = find_input_y + input_height + 8` → another `line_height`
- Replace input: `replace_input_y = replace_label_y + line_height + 4` → another `line_height + 8`

So total content height from top of modal:
- `8 + line_height + 8 + line_height + 4 + (line_height + 8) + 8 + line_height + 4 + (line_height + 8)`
- = `8 + lh + 8 + lh + 4 + lh + 8 + 8 + lh + 4 + lh + 8` = `5*lh + 48`

Base height = `3*lh + 20`, extra = `2*lh + 16`, total modal = `5*lh + 36`.

Content needs `5*lh + 48` but modal is only `5*lh + 36` — **12px short**. The replace input's bottom gets clipped.

*   **Bottom Edge**: While the main background fill is no longer clipped at the bottom, the structural outline (border) on the right side is not constrained to the modal's height.

### Detailed Edge Analysis
*   **Top Edge**: Clean, with a solid border matching the background.
*   **Left Edge**: Clean and aligned with the background fill.
*   **Right Edge**: Not clean. The background fill stops prematurely, and the border stroke is rendered at a different X-coordinate, continuing vertically into the editor space.
*   **Bottom Edge**: The background fill ends horizontally, but the right-side vertical stroke continues past the bottom-right corner.

### Overlap
*   The modal correctly sits on top of the code, but the "ghost" border line on the right creates an unintended visual overlap with the editor's text area.

```
In the rendering system, `line_height` and `char_width` are derived from font metrics within the `Renderer` and then synchronized to the `AppModel`.

### Key Findings
- **`char_width` calculation**: Derived from the `advance_width` of the 'M' character glyph for the current font and size.
- **`line_height` calculation**: Derived from `fontdue`'s `horizontal_line_metrics`, specifically using `line_metrics.new_line_size.ceil()`.
- **Initialization & Scaling**: The `Renderer::with_scale_factor` method performs these calculations whenever the app starts or the DPI scale factor changes. These values are then pushed into the `AppModel` via `set_char_width` and direct assignment in `src/runtime/app.rs`.
- **TextPainter**: It receives these pre-calculated metrics in its constructor to perform layout and drawing.

Relevant files:
- [src/view/mod.rs#L127-L165](file://~/code/token-editor/src/view/mod.rs#L127-L165) - `Renderer` calculates `char_width` and `line_height` from font metrics.
- [src/view/frame.rs#L330-L369](file://~/code/token-editor/src/view/frame.rs#L330-L369) - `TextPainter` initialization and metric getters.
- [src/runtime/app.rs#L203-L243](file://~/code/token-editor/src/runtime/app.rs#L203-L243) - Synchronization of metrics from `Renderer` to `AppModel`.
- [src/model/mod.rs#L591-L604](file://~/code/token-editor/src/model/mod.rs#L591-L604) - `AppModel::set_char_width` updates viewport dimensions.
- [src/model/mod.rs#L484-L501](file://~/code/token-editor/src/model/mod.rs#L484-L501) - Deriving UI element heights (like tab bar) from `line_height`.
```

## Assistant

Fixed. Two bugs resolved:

1. **Height clipping** — `extra_height` was 12px short (changed `+4` to `+16`)
2. **Border misalignment** — `draw_bordered_rect` was stamping the semi-transparent fill (`#181818E0`) as raw pixel values instead of blending, causing the opaque border to appear detached from the translucent background

The fix in `draw_bordered_rect` also benefits all other modals that use semi-transparent overlay backgrounds. Let me regenerate all screenshots that have modals and run tests:
